‘I’m lucky to have grown up around artists’: Cora Sheibani on her path to becoming a jeweller

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Cora Sheibani wearing jewellery from her own collections: a bronze bracelet (from Pottering Around collection), Renaissance earrings in platinum and a sapphire facet ring in 18K red gold (both from Facets & Forms Collection)

Art of Luxury magazine, published twice per year by The Art Newspaper, explores how grande marque fashion, jewellery, travel and lifestyle interact with artists, the art market and the museums and heritage sector.

The jeweller Cora Sheibani lives in London and works from a small studio in Mayfair. She was raised in the art world—her father was the celebrated Swiss dealer Bruno Bischofberger, who collaborated with artists including Jean-Michel Basquiat, Andy Warhol and Francesco Clemente. As a dyslexic, Cora did not embrace reading as a child, but now she explains why it is her window on the world.

For me, luxury is about having time. As you get older, and as the world gets faster, there’s never enough of it. In an ideal world I would travel more, both for work and for pleasure; and visit exhibitions, galleries and museums. But I also have a son who needs a lot of my attention—he is very autistic, with severe ADHD—and so I travel through books, and reading has become a way to experience culture, art and creativity.

I’m lucky to have grown up around artists and people who didn’t follow the conventional path. Francesco Clemente is a close family friend, and his daughters are more like my cousins. We’re close to the Schnabel family; Jean Tinguely was my sister’s godfather. So even though I barely read as a child—it was just too hard—I made myself study liberal arts before I became a jeweller. Only after university I started reading German literature. It’s my mother language, after all.

I’ve always loved jewellery. When I was 15, I worked in my parents’ gallery over the summer, and spent half my earnings on two rings, both bronze, one Greek, one Roman. Now I work primarily with gold: at first it was because yellow is my favourite colour, but you also learn that gold is the friendliest metal to handle.

It was hard to be taken seriously as a jeweller at the beginning. People thought it was a hobby, and I really had to be patient and persevere—that’s something I can do, that dyslexia taught me. Now I’m incredibly proud of my work and how successful the business has become.

Jewellery books and especially exhibition catalogues are a real inspiration. An essay by Amin Jaffer in the catalogue for Joyaux Dynastiques, a show at the Hôtel de la Marine in Paris last year of royal jewels, explained the meaning of a particular kind of nine-gem Indian jewel. It really helped me with a commission I’d just received from a client for a piece using nine different coloured stones.

I buy some early editions from Shapero Rare Books in Mayfair—I pass it every time I go from the gym to the studio and can’t resist taking a look. I just finished reading Crime and Punishment by Fyodor Dostoyevsky in its first English translation by Frederick Whishaw. It’s very rare, a real collector’s item. It’s generally thought that translations have improved, but there’s something special about reading a contemporaneous one. It’s a rich experience that takes you back in time.

Once a month, I get together with my book club. There’s only three of us, and we don’t all have to read the same book. Instead, we talk about what we are each reading. We meet, usually on a Friday morning, at Daria Buchman’s house, and have a breakfast of scrambled eggs. Daria, with her husband Dimitry, sponsors the International Booker Prize, so she is really on top of every new novel. She hadn’t read Beloved by Toni Morrison, though. I’d read three works that talked about slavery from different regional angles—Beloved, Crooked Plow by Itamar Vieira Junior and Ben Okri’s The Last Gift of the Master Artists—and recommended to my group they did the same. You begin to understand a bit better how the lives of people from one continent evolved in such different (and at times similar) ways. On the other hand, Daria made me read the wonderful Wuthering Heights. I wouldn’t have done it if she hadn’t insisted.

Frédéric Boucheron created the first necklace without a clasp so that women could put it on unaided

London-based Dutch artist uses reassembled broken china fragments to create five unique vessels

Douriean Fletcher, who made the jewellery for Black Panther, speaks at the National Museum of Women in the Arts

Called Serenissima, the jewels use finely wrought metals to capture the art of the lace makers found on the island of Burano

arts at CERN translate the mechanics of the universe into creative installations

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

art and physics meet through works that translate invisible particles, experimental data, and abstract dimensions into sensory experiences.

annalise kamegawa November 10, 2026 7 min

When asked, ‘What does CERN do?’ most people would probably scratch their heads and respond, ‘Physics.’ As the European Laboratory for Particle Physics in Geneva, it most certainly does physics, but another thing it does—alongside maintaining a Large Hadron Collider and developing inventions like the World Wide Web—is invite artists from around the world to produce work in tandem with the sciences. Through its artist residencies, commissions, and programs, it has invited the likes of Tomás Saraceno, Ryoji Ikeda, and other renowned artists to create works that, much like the laboratory’s research, demystify the world around us.

There’s a feeling that the principles of physics, especially the discoveries made during the 20th century, have unlocked the mysteries of the universe. Films like Oppenheimer (2023) and Interstellar (2014) attempt to show the power of these principles through atomic bombs, time dilation, and wormholes. Yet, when sitting in front of an equation like e=mc², how many of us know what to make of it? When peering into the wires, buttons, and lights of a laboratory, what signals make it through to the brain? This is where the power of art comes into play at CERN—not only can it help transform these ideas into comprehensible forms, but it can also, at times, suggest new applications for these scientific tools.

Take, for example, Julius von Bismarck’s 2023 sculpture, Round About Four Dimensions. The artist, who apprenticed at the Berlin Institute for Spatial Experiments, created a ‘tesseract,’ or essentially a ‘shadow’ of a four-dimensional cube. When describing the project—a webbed, cube-like structure made of aluminum, stainless steel, brass, polymers, and electronics—Arts at CERN says of von Bismarck’s work, ‘This three-dimensional revolving motion is a projection of a four-dimensional rotation, a phenomenon we cannot fully grasp. This previously intangible concept is brought to life by the kinetic tesseract sculpture, which wraps endlessly around itself.’

With 32 expanding and contracting lengths, or edges, the sculpture looks as though it swallows the form at its center or turns inside out. Using a series of software-programmed controls, the sculpture brings viewers closer to understanding the mechanics of four-dimensional objects.

CERN is largely known for being home to the Large Hadron Collider, a machine that launches protons at nearly the speed of light. With this machine, researchers can pursue different experiments, like ALICE, which studies, ‘the physics of strongly interacting matter at extreme energy densities, where a phase of matter called quark-gluon plasma forms.’ For their 2018 project HALO, the artistic group Semiconductor created a work that sought to illustrate the experiments behind ATLAS, a ‘general-purpose detector,’ or a series of cameras and documentation equipment inside the Large Hadron Collider that tracks the results of experiments conducted within it.

Commissioned by Audemars Piguet in collaboration with CERN, HALO was a cylindrical installation that visualized the flow of data from ATLAS in an inhabitable installation that recalled the actual tube-like form of the machine. During their 2015 residency at CERN, the group became familiar with how the machines worked. In the 2018 work, they took the mass of information coming from ATLAS and slowed it down into sounds and flashes of light that are perceivable to the human eye.

Art Basel 2018 – Semiconductor’s artwork titled “HALO” | Audemars Piguet

Alongside its commissions and exhibitions, CERN also organizes residencies that bring artists into contact with its advanced machinery, research facilities, and scientists. Most recently, in collaboration with the Nobel Prize Museum, it awarded Collide Stockholm, its international residency award, to Lithuanian-born artist Emilija Škarnulytė. CERN describes her practice as ‘rooted in the exploration of infrastructures that mediate between the visible and the invisible, the human and the post-human, the present and deep time.’ Through meditative moving images and immersive audiovisual installations, Škarnulytė explores the hidden systems and processes that shape the natural world, often shifting the viewer’s perspective beyond the limits of human perception.

Ultimately, what these collaborations at CERN reveal is that art and science are not such distant disciplines. Both are driven by a desire to understand what lies beyond the limits of human perception, whether that means exploring the smallest particles of matter, imagining dimensions we cannot physically experience, or uncovering the hidden infrastructures of the natural world. By translating complex scientific processes into sensory, spatial, and immersive encounters, artists offer us new ways of relating to the universe. More than simply making science accessible, these collaborations challenge our understanding of humanity’s place within it, suggesting that seeing the world differently may be the first step towards understanding it.

This article is part of designboom’s POSTHUMAN UTOPIAS chapter, exploring what happens when humans are no longer the measure of all things. These stories rethink our relationship with the planet through interspecies architecture, living materials, regenerative landscapes and emerging technologies – learn more here! The chapter is presented in partnership with INDX|GLOBAL – a global cultural platform placing Indian architecture, design, and creative intelligence in conversation with the world.

Bitcoin trading picked up, but sellers took the initiative

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin faces a key inflation test near $75,000 as Glassnode data shows sellers dominating spot trading and ETF outflows deepening.

Bitcoin's latest weekly trading data showed sellers retaining the initiative even as turnover rose, leaving its recovery near $75,000 without a clear sign that aggressive buyers were taking control.

The Nov. 9 report from blockchain analytics firm Glassnode sets a cautious starting point ahead of today's US inflation release.

Reuters reported closing gains of about 2.4% for the S&P 500 and 2.7% for the Nasdaq Composite on Nov. 9, with cheaper oil helping relieve inflation worries. Bitcoin was trading near $75,000 on Nov. 9, according to Glassnode.

Glassnode's latest Market Pulse put spot cumulative volume delta (CVD) at negative $92 million, compared with positive $7 million seven days earlier. Its spot volume indicator rose 13.3% to $6.4 billion from $5.6 billion.

CVD tracks the balance between aggressive buying and selling within the activity Glassnode measures. Negative CVD means aggressive selling outweighed aggressive buying in that measured activity.

The report's weekly indicators describe the demand conditions Bitcoin brought into the equity rally.

Glassnode's November 9 weekly snapshot shows spot activity rising while aggressive order flow turned negative, compared with seven days earlier.

Higher trading volume can accompany seller aggression. It does not by itself confirm renewed buying pressure.

Weekly report comparison, not Monday US-session data. Spot volume is an indicator, not a stated calendar-week total. Negative CVD concerns aggressive order flow within measured activity; every trade has a buyer. Separate scales do not compare dollar magnitudes across metrics.

Two separate linear bar scales use reported indicator values: spot volume 5.6 and 6.4 USD billions, CVD +6.6 and -91.8 USD millions. CVD bars share a zero baseline. Percentage change is Glassnode's reported 13.3%, not recomputed from rounded bar values.

Weekly report comparison, not Monday US-session data. Spot volume is an indicator, not a stated calendar-week total. Negative CVD concerns aggressive order flow within measured activity; every trade has a buyer. Separate scales do not compare dollar magnitudes across metrics.

Farside Investors' completed Nov. 2-6 sessions show four aggregate outflow days and one inflow day, totaling $1,282 million in net outflows. Another $201 million left the funds on Nov. 9.

Glassnode put futures open interest at $37 billion, down 1.1% from $37.4 billion seven days earlier, and described the unwind as partial. Funding payments remained elevated while short-term holders were near break-even.

A price recovery would carry more weight if improving spot CVD showed buyers taking the initiative, while positive ETF flows persisted across completed sessions.

In its Nov. 4 analysis, Glassnode placed the short-term holder cost basis at $75,700 and the True Market Mean at $77,700. The first estimates what newer holders paid, while the second estimates the cost basis of active investors.

Below, Glassnode identified a broader $73,000-$76,000 retest band and said a sustained break below $73,000 would point to longer consolidation.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics is scheduled to release October CPI today. A favorable inflation reading could ease rate expectations and support risk appetite. As the Federal Reserve explains, expected policy rates affect broader interest rates and financial conditions.

Reuters reported Bitcoin's late-Sunday fall to $73,708 amid investor worries that a Democratic-controlled Congress would toughen crypto regulation. Those concerns offer a separate reason for caution alongside the trading data.

AI is becoming an information gateway. Audiences still don’t want it making their news.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

An average of 24% of respondents across nine countries said they’d seen a label noting that content was made with AI in the last week, and 20% had seen a bullet-point AI news summary.

In my Nieman Lab prediction this time last year, I wrote that “in 2026, the use of AI as an information gateway will only rise further.”

As it turns out, I was right. In new research that I published with my colleagues Rasmus Kleis Nielsen and Richard Fletcher, one of our many findings is that generative AI systems are becoming ever more significant information intermediaries, both when intentionally used and when incidentally encountered.

For our new report, “Generative AI and News Report 2026: From Standalone Tools to Information Infrastructure,”  we surveyed audiences across nine countries, including the United States, on their use of AI in their everyday lives, what they think its impact will be on different sectors of society, and what they think about its use in news and journalism specifically. I obviously think you should read the whole report, but here are some of the major takeaways.

The use of standalone conversational AI systems such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini continues to grow rapidly, with 51% of respondents across the six countries where we have past data saying they now use AI weekly (49% in the U.S.). That’s a faster spread, at least in the U.S., than smartphones or the internet saw.

Many people also encounter AI through “incidental” AI exposure in the AI-infused services and platforms they’re already using. Search engines such as Google and Bing are key drivers: 57% of respondents across countries said they saw or engaged with AI-generated answers or summaries in the last week. News media, too, drives AI exposure. An average of 24% of respondents across nine countries said they’d seen a label noting that content was made with AI in the last week, and 20% had seen a bullet-point AI news summary.

Despite all the talk about AI agents, we find that, so far, they haven’t broken into the mainstream. The vast majority of respondents across all nine countries (67%) said they had not used an AI agent in the last week. Fourteen percent used one for a real task and 13% tried one just to experiment.

Our data provides solid evidence that a broader transformation of the information environment is afoot. Conversational AI systems have arrived as information intermediaries. Trust is not the main reason people use them. Instead, people see conversational AI systems as friction-reducing machines that get them information more quickly.

“Getting information” continues to be the most common use case. Thirty-eight percent of respondents across countries (35% in the U.S.) said they had used generative AI for getting information in the last week, up from 24% in 2025 and 11% in 2024.

We looked more closely at what people do when they say they use AI for getting information. Across the six countries we’ve surveyed since 2024, we primarily see a mixture of search-equivalent behaviors like “researching a topic” (24%) or “answering factual questions” (18%). People are also increasingly turning to AI for advice (20%).

Using AI directly for news, meanwhile, remains a niche activity, as we demonstrated in the Digital News Report earlier this year. But that use is increasing: 9% of respondents across our six core countries say they use AI for news, up from 6% in 2025 and 3% in 2024.

Many people don’t hold clear or strong views about whether AI output is trustworthy. But people who do rely on it for information trust it. Across the nine countries, 75% of those who used AI to answer factual questions trusted the answers, as did 76% of those who used it for advice and 72% of those who used it for news. Only small minorities expressed distrust.

At the same time, trust and trustworthiness do not, at this stage, seem to be central to why people rely on AI systems. Instead, people tend to see these systems as friction-reducing machines. The convenience of getting a faster and easier answer was chosen by 64% as the main reason they use AI, followed by the interactivity afforded by natural language interfaces (56%). Only a minority (21%) said they use AI because they get more reliable information from it than from other sources.

As AI systems become widely used, their growing popularity is likely to affect the use of legacy content offerings and digital platforms and services. As my coauthor Rasmus Kleis Nielsen put it on LinkedIn, rapid growth in AI use isn’t just eating into what news sites have to offer. It’s eating into search, too — with people who use AI for information saying they use not just news, but search engines less, too, and by a wider margin. Challenging times may be ahead not just for news publishers, but also for the incumbent digital giants previously seen as unassailable.

As in previous years, we wanted to know what people think of AI’s use in journalism and news. We find that people’s attitudes here are remarkably entrenched and negative — though they also haven’t worsened significantly.

People across countries remain uncomfortable with the use of AI in the newsroom, especially with the idea of news being made entirely or primarily with AI. On average across nine countries, just 12% of respondents say they are very or somewhat comfortable with news made entirely by AI. That figure rises to 19% if there is some human oversight. This has hardly changed since 2024, and the gap between human- and AI-made news appears in every country we surveyed.

This also remains true regardless of whether it is breaking news or reporting. People are even more uncomfortable with AI being used for commentary or opinion — formats where the expectation that a human should share their view is strongest.

Our findings raise several important questions for journalists and publishers navigating the rise of generative AI.

First, generative AI is becoming an increasingly significant information intermediaries, with a number of possible knock-on effects on news. People are using AI to reduce friction by getting “good enough” outputs for some information needs — and skipping legacy platforms, established publishers, and the time and effort it takes to find the best available information. Discovery, brand attribution, data collection, and monetization all grow more challenging for publishers in this context.

Second, even though use continues to grow, the public remains skeptical of AI in news. This isn’t just a challenge for newsrooms’ internal adoption. It also shows that publishers using AI need to communicate to audiences what’s in it for them.

Third, beyond news, our findings speak to wider debates about AI’s societal impact. As in other studies on this topic, we find that uptake of AI is uneven. Weekly use is more common among people in professional occupations than those in entry-level or service occupations. Most people rely on free — usually less advanced — AI models.

The differences in how — not just whether — people use AI may become a new dividing line. As AI researcher and OpenAI cofounder Andrej Karpathy recently argued, there is a widening “gap in shared understanding of LLM capability,” with most people not seeing what has left those inside AI firms both excited and worried.

Expectations of what AI will do differ far more between daily users and people who have never used it than between any other groups in our survey. When two groups form very different views of the same technology because they have such different starting points, there will likely be consequences.

This doesn’t matter just for people’s individual choices around whether and how to make use of AI in their own lives. It also matters where AI is adopted and governed through collective decisions — in public services, for example — that apply to those who do not use it.

Felix Simon is the research fellow in AI, information, and news at the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism and a research associate at the Oxford Internet Institute (OII) at the University of Oxford.

Felix Simon is the research fellow in AI, information, and news at the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism and a research associate at the Oxford Internet Institute (OII) at the University of Oxford.

Arsenal set price for Chelsea to sign Martin Zubimendi after Real Madrid approach

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Chelsea have revived their interest in Arsenal midfielder Martin Zubimendi ahead of the January transfer window but face competition from Real Madrid and Atletico Madrid.

The Blues were one of a number of clubs to express interest in signing Zubimendi last summer but the Spanish World Cup winner ended up staying at Arsenal.

Zubimendi enjoyed a bright start to his Arsenal career following a £60m move from Real Sociedad in 2025 but became a less influential figure in Mikel Arteta’s side towards the end of last season.

The 27-year-old appeared fatigued in the second-half of the campaign and lost his place in the team, with a then 19-year-old Myles Lewis-Skelly starting the Champions League final ahead of him.

Zubimendi saw the intense competition for places at the Emirates Stadium only increase in the summer as Arsenal spent £75m to sign Brazilian midfielder Bruno Guimaraes from Newcastle United.

As a result Zubimendi is still waiting for his first Premier League start of the season, with his lack of game-time sparking renewed speculation over his future in north London.

Zubimendi’s frustrating spell at Arsenal has not put off potential suitors, with Chelsea, Real Madrid and Atletico Madrid all ‘monitoring’ the Spaniard ahead of the January transfer window.

According to The Independent, all three clubs suspect a January deal could be possible and view Zubimendi as an ideal acquisition given he is now a Premier League, World Cup, European Championship and Nations League winner.

It is claimed Arteta still regards Zubimendi as a ‘valuable option’ given Arsenal expect to challenge for both the Premier League and Champions League yet again this term.

But Arsenal’s desire to sign a new forward in January may depend on a major sale and Zubimendi would be an obvious candidate to be moved on, although the English champions would want to recoup at least the £60m they paid for him 16 months ago.

Xabi Alonso is keen to bolster his options in midfield after Chelsea sold Enzo Fernandez to Manchester City on deadline day last summer and knows Zubimendi well from his time at Real Sociedad.

Jose Mourinho also intends to sign a new midfielder in January and Real Madrid have been huge admirers of Zubimendi ever since he broke through in La Liga.

But Real face competition from arch-rivals Atletico, with reports in Spain claiming Diego Simeone is ‘dreaming’ of signing Zubimendi from Arsenal.

Zubimendi came on as an injury-time substitute in Arsenal’s 1-0 win over Hull City on Saturday, a result that sent Arteta’s side six points clear at the top of the table.

Amex Platinum Card Bonus: As High As 175K Points Welcome Offer (Check Yours)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The American Express Platinum Card® is one of the most popular premium cards on the market, and it currently has an incredible welcome offer that’s as high as 175,000 bonus points, making it the ideal time to apply. In this post, I’d like to go over all the details of the current Amex Platinum bonus, including who is eligible, and why you might want to apply.

While the Amex Platinum has a steep annual fee, it also has compelling perks that will make it worthwhile for many, ranging from lounge access to credits. If you’re eligible for the current bonus, then picking up the card and giving it a try is a no brainer, in my opinion.

Link: Learn more about the American Express Platinum Card®

The Amex Platinum Card welcome offer is potentially very lucrative, with a twist. The Amex Platinum bonus is marketed as being as high as 175,000 Membership Rewards points after spending $12,000 within the first six months. However, Amex notes how the welcome offers vary, and applicants may not be eligible for that welcome offer.

The way this works, you can apply for the card, and then find out your offer amount. Then you can decide whether you want to accept the Amex Platinum welcome offer or not. That’s an unconventional approach for a welcome offer, though I appreciate that Amex has the “apply with confidence” feature, so there won’t be a hard pull unless you accept the offer.

Based on my latest miles & points valuations, I consider Membership Rewards points to be worth 1.7 cents each. That means I value 175,000 points at a staggering $2,975, making it among the best welcome offers out there. Of course that assumes you’re eligible for this Amex Platinum bonus, which not everyone will be.

The spending requirement is significant, though you do have six months to complete it, so averaging $2,000 per month in spending might not be that hard for many people. There are lots of creative ways to reach minimum spending requirements, including things like paying quarterly taxes by credit card, if eligible.

This is potentially one of the best welcome offers that we’ve ever seen on the card, and publicly available welcome offers don’t get much better than this. In some cases people may be targeted for even bigger offers, but it’s rare. To maximize your odds of getting a targeted offer, make sure you’re opted in to getting marketing communications (by email and mail) from Amex.

There are lots of great uses for Amex points, as they can be transferred to roughly 20 airline and hotel partners, and those are the best ways to get outsized value. These partners range from Air France-KLM Flying Blue, to Emirates Skywards, to Singapore Airlines KrisFlyer. Just to give a couple of Amex redemption examples:

In line with Amex’s “once in a lifetime” rule, the Amex Platinum Card bonus isn’t available to those who currently have the card, or those who have had the card (or previous versions of the card) in the past. On top of that, Amex’s general card restrictions apply. There are a few additional things to note:

The Amex Platinum Card has a $895 annual fee (Rates & Fees). The primary reason you’ll want to get the card is because of the extensive perks that it offers, ranging from airport lounge access, to elite status, to credits.

NYSE Selects Avalanche for Tokenized Stock Settlement: Can AVAX Reclaim $9.00?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Home » Altcoins » NYSE Selects Avalanche for Tokenized Stock Settlement: Can AVAX Reclaim $9.00?

Wall Street’s tokenization push gives Avalanche an institutional boost, but AVAX technicals demand caution at important level.

In a major endorsement of enterprise blockchain infrastructure, the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) has chosen Avalanche (AVAX) technology as the core ledger for clearing and settling trades on its planned tokenized stock platform.

According to an Ava Labs statement, Avalanche will provide the core ledger technology for the clearing firm of the NYSE-affiliated digital trading platform.

Subject to regulatory approval, the platform is designed to enable 24/7 trading, clearing and onchain settlement of tokenized securities.

It will support equities issued across multiple blockchains, while Avalanche will provide the underlying settlement infrastructure, offering predictable performance and costs, controlled network access and regulatory flexibility.

The announcement comes amid intensifying competition in financial asset tokenization. The NYSE has expanded its transfer agent network to six firms, including Broadridge, Computershare and Superstate.

Nasdaq is also developing tokenized securities infrastructure, while DTCC launched its tokenization service in October, using a private Besu blockchain and the Canton Network.

Meanwhile, the SEC’s new innovation exemption is opening the door to tokenized equity trading, with OKX and ICE pursuing a separate initiative.

Following the announcement, AVAX broke out of its previous consolidation range but faced resistance at $8.80–$9.00, triggering a pullback. As of November 10, AVAX is trading at approximately $8.48, up 7.2% over 24 hours.

The rally pushed Bollinger Bands wider, signaling increased volatility. AVAX is now trading below the middle band at $8.62 and the moving-average reference at $8.56, suggesting that upward momentum has cooled.

Resistance stands at $8.60–$8.65 and $9.00–$9.28, while key support can be located around $8.00, followed by $7.70 and $7.30.

Over the next few days, AVAX’s direction will largely depend on whether buyers can reclaim $8.65.A recovery above $8.65 could put AVAX back on course to test $9.00. A break above $9.28, supported by stronger trading volume, would signal renewed bullish momentum.

Failure to regain near-term resistance could leave the token consolidating, while a break below $7.95 would expose lower support levels at $7.70 or $7.30.

The NYSE announcement strengthens Avalanche’s position in institutional tokenization, but its implications for AVAX remain less direct. The platform is expected to run on a dedicated blockchain built using Avalanche technology rather than the public Avalanche network.

The extent of any resulting demand for AVAX will depend on how the platform is implemented and whether it creates mechanisms for value to accrue to the token.

A sustained recovery could put $9.50–$10.00 within reach, with further resistance around $10.50–$11.50. However, failure to overcome overhead resistance could leave AVAX in a broader consolidation range.

The NYSE announcement is a significant development for Avalanche’s institutional prospects, but it has yet to translate into a confirmed bullish reversal for AVAX. After the initial AVAX price rally, traders are now assessing whether buying demand can sustain the move beyond the immediate resistance level.

Coinbase, Kalshi join lawsuit backing CFTC over US stock perp futures

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Coinbase, Kalshi and four other companies backed the CFTC in a lawsuit over whether US exchanges can list perpetual futures tied to individual stocks.

Coinbase, Kalshi and four other companies asked a federal court Monday to accept a brief backing the United States Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) in a lawsuit over perpetual futures tied to individual US stocks.

According to a proposed amicus brief shared Monday by Coinbase legal representative Molly Abraham, the group also includes Kraken parent Payward, trading companies DRW Holdings and Jump Trading, and crypto investment company Paradigm Operations. The companies argue that stock perpetual futures fall under existing US futures laws.

The filing comes days after a federal judge found that the CFTC likely “exceeded its statutory authority” in allowing single-stock perpetuals to trade as security futures.

The case could determine whether US exchanges can offer retail traders contracts that track individual stocks without expiring.

On Nov. 3, the CFTC announced that single-stock perpetual futures, or stock perps, could begin trading on US-regulated exchanges for the first time.

The Capital Markets Alliance Association (CMAA), a newly formed trade and advocacy group representing traditional financial market participants and whose members include Cboe and Citadel Securities, sued the CFTC last week, challenging the CFTC’s authority to allow the contracts to trade.

Stock perps allow traders to speculate on the price movements of individual stocks without owning the shares. Unlike traditional futures, they have no fixed expiration date.

The CMAA argues that because the contracts lack a fixed expiration date, they should be regulated as security-based swaps, a type of derivative linked to stocks or other securities that is primarily overseen by the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) and faces tighter restrictions on retail trading.

The six companies backing the CFTC disagree, arguing that a fixed expiration date is not required for a contract to qualify as a security future, which falls under joint CFTC and SEC oversight.

Related: Coinbase’s US national trust bank charter takes effect

Following the CMAA’s complaint, US District Judge Valerie Caproni said the plaintiff was likely to succeed in its claim that the CFTC exceeded its statutory authority by allowing stock perps to trade as security futures.

Caproni found that the contracts appeared to qualify as swaps rather than security futures, casting doubt on the agency’s legal interpretation.

However, she denied CMAA’s request for a temporary restraining order, finding that the group had failed to demonstrate imminent, irreparable harm. The decision left the CFTC’s trading approvals in place while the lawsuit proceeds.

CMAA is scheduled to file its reply to the CFTC’s opposition to a preliminary injunction on Tuesday.

Anthropic sets IPO price range of $70 to $78, looks to start trading Nov. 19, sources say

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Anthropic said it will offer 825 million shares in its upcoming IPO at a price range between $70 to $78 per share, according to an updated filing on Tuesday, bringing the five-year-old artificial intelligence lab a step closer to a potentially historic Nasdaq debut.

The company is planning to price on Nov. 18 and trade on Nov. 19, according to people familiar with the details who were granted anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is targeting retail allocation of about 20%, the people said, a much higher percentage than the typical IPO, which is 5% to 10% for retail investors.

At the high end of the range, Anthropic would net up to $64.35 billion in proceeds through the IPO. The company could end up being worth up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis, up from the $965 billion valuation it announced as part of a funding round in May.

Anthropic said it has entered into an agreement with Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company to purchase $26 billion of the company's Class N common stock in a private placement at a price per share equal to the IPO price. The shares sold in the private placement will not be registered in the offering and will not be subject to lock-up agreements with the underwriters, the company said.

As Anthropic executives get set to pitch the offering to investors, they have to contend with a tumultuous political environment, following last week's midterm elections that included a Democratic sweep in Congress and the passage of numerous statewide ballot measures that could slow the AI buildout. They're also facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade on concern that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for companies that need it for costly infrastructure purchases.

CNBC reported last week that some Anthropic investors were bracing for a lower valuation than had previously been expected. While Anthropic had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation until recently, shareholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

Should Anthropic reach the Nasdaq this month as planned, under ticker symbol "ANT," the debut would come roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. Elon Musk's reusable rocket and AI company has a market cap of just under $2 trillion, around its valuation at the time of the IPO.

Institutional demand for Anthropic is even stronger than it was for SpaceX at this point in the process, the people said. The company's book is already covered at the $70 to $78 per share range on an allocable basis, they said.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the AI lab in 2021 after he and a small group of researchers defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and the company primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

On Monday, Anthropic introduced two new AI models, Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, the first time the company has released so-called "frontier" models since Amodei called to slow down the pace of AI development in September. Anthropic said the models went through rigorous safety and alignment testing, and that the company developed new evaluations and post-launch monitoring tools ahead of the release.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic said in its amended filing on Tuesday that its run-rate revenue was roughly $81 billion at the end of October, up $10 billion from the previous month.

"Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles," Anthropic's co-founders wrote in the prospectus. "Everyone who owns our stock, whether an institution or an individual, becomes part of that effort. We hope you will share our ambition to see it though."

Brit Arrested After Flight Attendant Finds Hidden Camera In Airplane Bathroom, Now Faces Voyeurism And Cover-Up Charges

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A flight attendant discovered a hidden camera while using the lavatory on a Scoot flight from Japan to Singapore. A British passenger now faces charges of voyeurism and obstructing justice, with police alleging that he remotely activated the camera and later tried to erase evidence after realizing it had been found.

Hugh Lutley, a 52-year-old British national, was charged on November 5, 2026, following an incident aboard Scoot flight TR821 from Osaka (KIX) to Singapore (SIN) on October 7.

He faces one charge each of voyeurism and obstruction of justice. It appears Lutley intends to plead guilty, was offered bail of S$10,000, and has a pretrial conference scheduled for November 17 (which of course is not a conviction or a sentence).

The Singapore Police Force’s account describes an alleged attempt to record people inside an aircraft toilet. Police say the passenger concealed a camera in the lavatory, left, and activated its recording function through an application on his phone.

A cabin crew member subsequently used the lavatory and discovered the device. She removed it and immediately alerted a colleague. The passenger allegedly returned to retrieve the camera, found it missing, and deleted the application and related photographs from his phone in an attempt to avoid detection.

Police detained him when the flight arrived at Singapore Changi Airport and seized his phone and the camera.

Those are statutory maximums, but the alleged attempt to destroy evidence exposes him to a charge carrying a substantially longer maximum prison term than the underlying voyeurism charge.

There is something particularly vile about placing a camera in an aircraft lavatory. Passengers and crew have little choice about where to go, and closing and locking the door should allow them to expect privacy.

I am thankful the flight attendant found the device and reported it. Discovering something like that while using the restroom must be deeply unsettling.

The published accounts do not establish how many people, if any, were recorded before the camera was discovered…the alleged installation and remote operation are disturbing enough.

Is this world so twisted that passengers must become amateur surveillance experts every time they use an airplane bathroom? If you notice an unfamiliar device or something suspicious, alert the crew promptly…yeah, I guess you should look around before you drop your drawers.

A British passenger faces criminal charges after a flight attendant found a hidden camera in a Scoot lavatory. Police allege he remotely operated it and then deleted material from his phone after discovering the camera had been removed.

The case remains before the court. For now, credit to the crew member who discovered the device and acted. An tight aircraft lavatory may offer very little comfort, but privacy ought to be a given.

A 3,300Km Walk Across Japan in Winter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Japanese publisher Katsunari Tanaka has set himself the challenge of hauling a handcart 3,345km from Japan’s northernmost tip to the southern edge of Okinawa in winter.

Tanaka, 49, set off from Cape Sōya in Hokkaido on October 7 and is heading south to Cape Hedo in Okinawa. He hopes to complete the traverse in 100 days over five months. As he will have to contend with some challenging winter conditions along the way, he has described it as “playing chase with winter.”

Rather than carrying everything on his back, he is pulling a handcart that weighs around 100kg. He also has a support team with a vehicle in case of emergencies. At some points, different members of his team have walked with him for a day or so. His daily target is around 35km, with rest days and organized stops built into the schedule.

The first few weeks have already brought strong headwinds, rain, and some steep climbs, making progress difficult at times. Temperatures have dropped close to freezing. At the end of October, he crossed a mountain pass between Yoichi and Iwanai. There, headwinds stirred up the gravel as he walked. Then the snow began. In the end, he took refuge with his support team until the weather improved.

Despite the conditions, Tanaka reached Hakodate Harbor on November 3, having covered 621km across Hokkaido. From Hakodate, he is crossing the Tsugaru Strait by ferry to Aomori, then will continue south along the Sanriku coast to Sendai.

From there, he follows the Pacific coast through Fukushima to Tokyo before joining the Tokaido route toward Osaka before heading west to Kyushu. A second ferry crossing brings him to Okinawa, where he will walk to his final destination, Cape Hedo.

Along a road in Hokkaido. Photo: Katsunari Tanaka/Instagram

This is not Tanaka’s first experience of traveling Japan with a handcart. Around a decade ago, he spent three years visiting all 47 prefectures, selling books directly to people he met along the way. Loading his publications onto a cart, he covered the country between 2013 and 2016, eventually selling 13,477 books. At the time, many people questioned what he was doing and whether books could really be sold on the street. But he says the experience shaped his approach to business and his belief in the power of personal connections.

Tanaka insists that he is not an adventurer. Instead, he is traveling the country as a “merchant.” This time, he is making the journey to support his mentor, Arthur Hollands, a Japanese evangelist known for undertaking lengthy walks while carrying a large wooden cross. Along the route, Tanaka plans to stop at 11 locations to give lectures. He welcomes people to join him for sections of the walk.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

Why the UEFA Nations League matters this month - and what it means for England, Scotland, Wales and co

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Knockout spots, relegation dogfights, and crucial seeding: why this week's Nations League action matters far more than you think

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Forget the notion of glorified friendlies, this month quarter-final berths, relegation peril, and major tournament seeding is all on the line in the UEFA Nations League.

The final round of group fixtures is anything but an international break distraction.

Here, we break down what it all means for the home nations.

The top two finishers in each League A group advance to a two-legged quarter-final stage, rather than just the group winners qualifying directly for a final-four tournament, as has previously been the case.

This week’s fixtures decide which nations book their place in the knockout bracket to compete for the trophy, while also dictating important top-seed status for the subsequent knockout draw.

The margins between tiers carry significant weight. Finishing top of the group in Leagues B, C, and D earns automatic promotion to the tier above, while finishing bottom of Leagues A, B, and C results in automatic relegation.

Crucially, the introduction of two-legged promotion/relegation play-offs - which will pit third-placed teams in League A against runners-up from League B, and third-placed teams in League B against runners-up from League C - means almost every matchday result directly determines either immediate survival, a climb up the ladder, or a drop into lower-tier fixtures.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Nations League standings double as an insurance policy for future European Championship and FIFA World Cup cycles.

Overall tournament rankings determine the seeding pots for upcoming European qualifying draws, directly affecting the difficulty of qualification paths. The highest-ranked Nations League group winners who fail to secure qualification through standard qualifying groups are handed secondary lifelines via tournament play-off berths.

England currently sit second in their group with nine points, trailing Spain (12 points) but comfortably ahead of Croatia (3 points) and Czechia (0 points).

Because the top two teams in League A advance, Thomas Tuchel's side have already effectively secured their quarter-final berth. The focus now shifts to whether they can overtake Spain for the group's top seed.

Thursday, Nov 12: vs Croatia (H – Wembley Stadium, London) - 19:45 GMT

Sunday, Nov 15: vs Spain (A – Metropolitano Stadium, Madrid) - 19:45 GMT

Wales are third in their top-tier group, trailing Portugal and Denmark, but sitting just above Norway. To avoid a relegation play-off against a League B runner-up or automatic relegation if they drop to fourth, they need results this week to either secure their safety or mount a late push into the top two for a quarter-final spot.

Saturday, Nov 14: vs Norway (A – Ullevaal Stadium, Oslo) - 17:00 GMT

Tuesday, Nov 17: vs Portugal (H – Cardiff City Stadium, Cardiff) - 19:45 GMT

Scotland find themselves in third place behind Switzerland and Slovenia, with North Macedonia rooted to the bottom. Finishing third in League B leads to a relegation play-off against a League C side, so the Tartan Army will be keen to pick up points and push for second place (promotion play-off) or even top spot (automatic promotion).

Friday, Nov 13: vs North Macedonia (H – Hampden Park, Glasgow) - 19:45 GMT

Monday, Nov 16: vs Switzerland (A – Kybunpark, St. Gallen) - 19:45 GMT

Michael O'Neill's side are in an excellent position, currently top of their group ahead of Hungary, Ukraine, and Georgia. If they maintain their first-place standing over the next two games, they will earn automatic promotion to League A.

Saturday, Nov 14: vs Ukraine (H – Windsor Park, Belfast) - 19:45 GMT

Tuesday, Nov 17: vs Hungary (A – Puskas Arena, Budapest) - 19:45 GMT

The Republic of Ireland sit in third on five points, trailing group leaders Austria and second-placed Kosovo. They will need strong performances in this window to climb into the top two for a shot at promotion, or risk facing a relegation play-off to retain their League B status.

The Best Travel Gifts for Platinum-Level Frequent Fliers

Osprey Mountain Bound Ski & Snowboard Boot Backpack

Savvy travelers know that good gear won’t rescue a bad itinerary, but it can remove a ton of friction from a trip. The right bag gets you through the airport faster, and smart organization means you spend less time digging through your luggage and more time enjoying the destination you went to all that trouble to get to.

These gifts are for travelers who have logged enough miles to know exactly what earns precious space in a carry-on.

Electric cooler brand Dometic packed a remarkable amount of engineering into its first drinkware launch. The Dometic Tumbler 350’s body uses two layers of 90 percent recycled 304 stainless steel around a vacuum gap with a copper liner that makes it an insulating beast. The Swig Lid’s dual gaskets prevent leaks, while a magnetic flip cap stays out of the way when you drink.

I’m willing to gamble on a lot of things while traveling internationally, but tap water is not one of them. LifeStraw’s Sip is a 10-inch stainless steel filter straw weighing less than 2 ounces, with a 0.2-micron membrane that removes bacteria, parasites, microplastics, silt, and sand. The filter lasts for up to 1,000 liters and requires no batteries or pumping. It doesn’t make chemically contaminated or salt water safe to drink, but for uncertain drinking water situations it’s a smart backup plan.

Christmas socks are a punchline—until they stop your calves from feeling like sausages after a 10-hour flight. Bombas uses graduated 15–20 mmHg compression to provide support through the lower leg during long stretches of sitting but keeps the feel much closer to a comfortable everyday sock than medical hosiery.

Thirty to forty ounces of beverage is a serious commitment, particularly when it sits in an indestructible, double-walled insulated YETI vessel. All that heft is offset by an easy-to-sip straw lid that seals when closed to eliminate supersized spills. This hog can travel, too, thanks to its tapered bottom that fits most vehicle cup holders despite its enormous upper body.

Traveling with my daughter has taught me that a carefully packed suitcase can become a yard sale 90 seconds after reaching a hotel. No Reception Club’s Sliders make it immediately obvious where everything went thanks to their transparent front windows, and color-coded zippers help you keep quick track of what goes where. The largest cube expands to fit larger items, while the smaller bags organize chargers, medicine, tiny precious toys, snacks, toiletries, or whatever other chaos you bring along.

Utah is home to some of the best resorts, skiing, and snow in the world, and there’s no better place to access all manner of winter fun than Heber Valley. Nestled in the mountains just south of Park City, Heber Valley offers fresh terrain—whether you’re on skis, snowshoes, or a sled—and lodging options for every type of stay. To days full of adventure and cozy nights in, this is the place for your next winter escape.

At just 460 grams, Fjällräven’s High Coast Foldsack is light and packable enough to disappear into a larger piece of luggage. Once you arrive, it turns into a hearty 24-liter daypack. Recycled waterproof polyamide makes up the body, while a tougher 400-denier Bergshell reinforces the base. There’s room for a 15-inch laptop, and removable chest and hip straps add support for longer walks. This is the bag for someone who wants to bring a backpack without dedicating half a suitcase to one or bringing it as a personal item.

Sitting in a middle seat for five hours is unpleasant enough without wearing rigid pants. These travel pants by Roark, on the other hand, feature a four-way-stretch Embarq fabric, a hybrid TSA-friendly waistband, zippered travel pockets, and laser-perforated ventilation across the rear to deliver comfort in the least comfortable situations. The straight cut and 31-inch inseam are what really sell them, because they look like normal pants you’d rock at your final destination without looking like you’re dressed for an expedition or lounging on your couch.

The most interesting part of Teva’s Aventrail Road-2-Trail is what’s missing: laces. A patented W Strapping System locks the foot down with multiple adjustment points and FuseLock hook-and-loop closures. Underfoot, HYPER-COMF cushioning combines responsive supercritical foam with supportive EVA, while the Spider Rubber Endure outsole uses 3.5-millimeter lugs for traction on pavement, gravel, and dirt. Add a breathable engineered-mesh upper and this is one running shoe that can handle any adventure.

While draping ski boots by their power straps over the top of a backpack looks cool, it is a suboptimal system. Osprey’s 45-liter Mountain Bound pack puts boots, helmet, goggles, and accessories inside dedicated compartments, then carries the whole mess with an AirScape backpanel and padded harness. Its recycled 420-denier NanoTough shell is highly water resistant for snowy parking lots. Even better, an included standing mat means you don’t have to put a clean sock directly onto said snowy parking lot while changing.

The Davis has been my go-to travel pack for years because of its rugged durability, handsome looks, and roll-top entry, which allows me to bring more home with me than I departed with. North St. cuts and sews the Davis in Portland, Oregon, and it was clearly built for Portland weather. The roll-top main compartment is fully waterproof and expands from roughly 14 liters closed to 20 liters open. A water-resistant exterior zipper gives quicker access to small stuff, while side pockets fit bottles or a U-lock. It is both minimal and comfortable with padded shoulder straps and a sternum strap that add enough structure for all-day adventures.

For all but the most disciplined cyclists, mountain bike gear pretty much always spreads across an entire vehicle. Thule’s 70-liter RoundTrip gives your helmet, shoes, goggles, clothes, tools, and nutrition distinct homes instead. It’s also easy to pack thanks to a spring-wire opening that props the entire duffel wide open, turning it into a gear bin instead of a dark cave.

Peak Design essentially hid a second backpack inside this one. The 40-liter system splits into a 34-liter main bag and a legitimate 16-liter daypack once you reach your destination. That means you can haul clothes and travel gear through the airport, drop the bulk of it at the hotel, and head out for the day without packing another bag. Peak Design’s obsessive organization compartments keep smaller gear from disappearing into the abyss along the way.

Peak Design’s Roller Pro is, well, peak carry-on tech. The Roller Pro’s SlimDrive handle uses slender 7-millimeter carbon-fiber tubes that reclaim interior space normally occupied by bulky rails. Neither hardshell nor softshell, the Roller Pro hides a rigid polycarbonate frame beneath recycled 550-denier Versa Shell fabric, combining protection with a softer, less industrial exterior. Replaceable 60-millimeter wheels and thoughtful laptop storage make this the bag my wife and I fight over using when our travel schedules overlap.

Coutinho 2.0? Liverpool make their move for Brazilian starlet after recent contract decision

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Almost 14 years on from signing Philippe Coutinho, Liverpool are reportedly making moves to potentially sign another Brazilian playmaker who’s been attracting some high-profile European interest.

The Reds’ former number 10 spent five years at Anfield, during which time he scored 54 goals in 201 appearances and was a hugely influential figure on the pitch, frequently conjuring up moments of majesty.

He was sold in January 2018 and wasn’t around for the peak years of the Jurgen Klopp reign, although there’s an up-and-coming talent from Brazil who LFC will be hoping can become synonymous with a successful era under Andoni Iraola.

According to Sport Witness, Liverpool have made official enquiries with Palmeiras about 19-year-old attacking midfielder Erick Bele, with the Brazilian club prepared to start negotiations at €30m (£25m).

Paris Saint-Germain have also been in contact regarding the teenager, who signed a contract extension to 2031 with his current employers last week.

The youngster stood out during a recent training camp for Brazil under-20s in which he provided three assists across wins over Denmark, United States and Mexico.

Bele has an impressive record at youth level for Palmeiras, with 20 goals in 45 games for their under-20 side, although his development is being managed carefully as he’s made just seven senior appearances so far (Transfermarkt).

He plays mostly as a number 10 but can also be deployed on the right flank, similar to Harvey Elliott, and it attests to how highly his club value him that they’ve rewarded him with such a lengthy new contract.

An analysis from Future Ballers cites finishing, pace, dribbling, technique, heading, off-the-ball movement and creativity among his strongest attributes, marking him out as a well-rounded young talent who also boasts plenty of physical strength.

Even if Liverpool were to sign Bele in 2027, realistically he wouldn’t be challenging for a first-team place straight away, given his inexperience at senior level and the presence of Florian Wirtz in the Reds’ XI.

A loan move or two to clubs in mainland Europe would likely be arranged in order to enhance his development, but we can certainly see why Anfield chiefs have enquired about an exciting prospect who, in time, could have strong echoes of Coutinho.

Google’s new AI tool just proved Signal encryption really works – here’s how

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

With the aim of securing other vital systems, Google DeepMind plans to open-source its AlphaProof Code.

Researchers at Google DeepMind used AI to formally verify portions of Signal‘s underlying code — meaning they’ve mathematically proven that at least parts of the encrypted messaging app’s code do what they say, without errors. That’s reassuring given how many vulnerabilities AI systems have discovered in software lately, and how many people’s communications are protected by Signal’s cryptography. WhatsApp and Google Messages use the same encryption.

Also: How AI security failures are endangering your supply chain

DeepMind used AlphaProof Code, “a novel agentic framework for formal verification at unprecedented depth that combines human expertise with AI agents,” the researchers said. They added that formal verification “is the highest standard of security assurance,” but, without automation at scale like AI systems, difficult and time-consuming.

AI companies like OpenAI have been on a math-breakthrough tear as of late, in some cases solving nearly century-old math problems with AI. By computing at such vast scale, AI models can rapidly accelerate much of the manual work mathematicians do.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Using AlphaProof Code and building on earlier verification work by the Signal Foundation, DeepMind researchers divided the verification task into two camps. Humans worked with agents to identify what they needed to prove, and multiple agents running Gemini Carbon, the company’s latest model, then executed it. The agents also audited the proof and “highlighted areas that needed a second look by a human — just 1% of the codebase, a massive time savings,” the researchers wrote.

Also: After the vibe-coding rush comes the debugging hangover

For good measure, researchers tested AlphaProof Code’s ability to distinguish correct from flawed code by showing it two previously fixed Signal bugs, for which it produced correct proofs.

“Our work marks the first time AI has been used to formally verify a widely deployed cryptographic protocol at this depth,” the researchers wrote. “It’s also the first time the implementation of a widely deployed protocol like this has been formally verified by any method, with or without AI.”

Normally, researchers need to manually develop a proof to verify software, which takes much longer. That labor-intensive barrier has made formal verification a distant promised land, until now. The DeepMind team’s verification took a month to do what might normally take experts years.

DeepMind did not verify all of Signal’s code, but noted that this test is a step toward improved security infrastructure at a time when AI is disrupting the entire cybersecurity space.

Also: How to encrypt emails in Gmail, Outlook, and more

“Having the core Signal protocol rigorously verified with automated theorem proving gives us even greater confidence that our code delivers on its privacy guarantees,” the researchers wrote.

The breakthrough reiterates how deeply AI is both a massive risk for cybersecurity and an equally powerful fix. The year 2026 has been especially marked by AI-related security incidents, many of which involve agents. At the same time, companies like Anthropic, Google, and OpenAI are developing models geared specifically toward cybersecurity, including Anthropic’s Claude Mythos.

Also: How to easily encrypt your files on an Android phone

DeepMind did not share any plans to release its findings as a consumer product, though the researchers are open-sourcing their code to help others with formal verification efforts. “Verifying Signal’s core cryptography lays the groundwork to secure other vital systems, from medical device firmware to power grid software,” DeepMind added

‘Home Is Where You Spend’: How Your Neighborhood Shapes Your Buying Habits

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Your home isn't just where you live; it's also where you spend the majority of your money, a new study from financial technology platform Bilt has found.

Bilt partnered with 451 Research and S&P Global on the 2026 State of the Neighborhood survey, aimed at exploring consumer spending habits as it doubles down on American business with the launch of Bilt Neighborhood OS and the expansion of its membership platform.

Bilt Neighborhood OS is an AI operating system and commerce network designed to unlock America's $6.6 trillion neighborhood commerce opportunity to help businesses grow without replacing their existing systems, the company says.

In the consumer spending survey, around two-thirds of the 6,951 survey participants conducted at least 80% of their "everyday spending" within 15 miles of their homes, and 92% of those surveyed planned on spending the same or more at local businesses in the coming months.

That's not surprising, given the results of a recent Re/Max survey, which found that 29% of prospective homeowners valued proximity to grocery stores and shopping, while 20% said they valued having restaurants and cafes nearby.

More than half of all survey respondents said future neighborhood development, including new housing or retail development, was "very important" to how they chose where to buy a home.

The results of the Bilt survey spell good news for small businesses in popular residential areas, suggesting that physical stores close to dense residential neighborhoods may continue to thrive in the age of online shopping.

"The disconnect between what people talk about versus where people actually spend is quite significant," Ankur Jain, founder and CEO of Bilt, tells Realtor.com®. "When you you look at the stats, all online shopping in the U.S. is $1.3 trillion a year. But offline, small- and medium-business local retail and food spending is $6.6 trillion."

On a quarterly basis, according to the U.S. Census Bureau, that broke down to $340.2 billion in online spending in the second quarter, making up 17.1% of total consumer spending.

Bilt's study found that 70% of food and grocery spending is still made in person.

Consumers spent an average of $375 on in-person grocery shopping each month. They spent an additional $175 on in-person dining and takeout and another $175 on in-person general shopping. Study participants reported spending an average of $50 each on entertainment and recreation; health and personal care; and transportation.

Sixty-six percent of survey respondents said they were were actively looking to support local businesses over big-box and online retailers and were willing to spend more to support them.

A recent report on consumer spending habits around the holidays from research firm Epsilon found that Gen Z in particular values in-store options for gift shopping. And while online shopping is still, overall, the preferred method for gift shopping, the study found that consumers across all generatios are doing the majority of their food and decoration shopping in person. Furthermore, they spent about an equal amount of their holiday clothing budgets online and in store.

One possible reason for the continued primacy of in-person shopping is consumers' paradoxical relationship with artificial intelligence marketing.

Research from McKinsey found that AI continues to drive sales and that AI usage in completing purchases climbed 70% year over year. However, younger, more tech-native shoppers "tend to be more skeptical of AI on average," the report found. (Sixty-seven percent of American Gen Z AI users trust AI chat and search tools versus the global average of 73%.)

The study was conducted in advance of Bilt launching its new offerings. Alongside Bilt Neighborhood OS, the Bilt Membership Platform is an expansion of the company's existing membership and loyalty technology. The company says it will enable organizations to launch their own branded membership programs via white-labeling or extend existing loyalty programs into the neighborhood.

"We are betting pretty big on the neighborhood versus online commerce," Jain says. "There is a huge gap between what the media and the Silicon Valley world talk about versus how real Americans are living. People talk about AI as 'How are we going to do online commerce AI and replace coding and jobs and all this stuff.'

"The truth is, the biggest beneficiaries of AI can be the local mom and pop businesses who don't have the ability to hire 2,000 people to go compete with, like, a Walmart or Amazon online.

"Everything is tied together. The more you can invest in the neighborhood, the more value you create in the housing. The more housing you have, the more you can invest in the neighborhood," he adds.

Bilt also announced a coalition of partners spanning payments, point-of-sale technology, reservations, and commerce platforms. Partners include Altamarea Group, AM Parking, Bungalow Hospitality Management, ByNext, FlyBox, Lettuce Entertain You, Lowe’s, Prince Street Hospitality Group, The Dinex Group, Thumbtack, among others.

Julie Gerstein is a writer and editor based in Philadelphia. She previously worked at Business Insider as an executive editor covering news, entertainment, and digital culture, and was the company's founding Singapore bureau chief. She was also a senior editor at BuzzFeed, covering lifestyle, fashion, and beauty. She earned a B.A. in women's studies from the University of Pennsylvania and an M.A. in media and cultural studies from the University of Sussex.

Nintendo Switch Players Will Soon Be Able To Gift Digital Games, Kinda

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A new Switch system update later this month is finally bringing us the ability to gift games via the Nintendo eShop, albeit with some hefty restrictions.

Digital games are increasingly becoming the norm across the games industry, yet strangely the concept of gifting digital games has yet to be widely adopted across all storefronts. While Steam and many PC platforms have supported the ability to send digital games to friends for at least a decade, on console only Microsoft allows Xbox players to do the same. Without it, people have simply had to make do with digital store cards. For Nintendo platforms, however, the company are promising to slightly change that and allow directly gifting games, âjust in time for the holidays.â

The feature, added in firmware update 23.5.0 and launching November 16, will allow players within a family group on Nintendo Switch to gift digital games to one another via the eShop. Once the feature has been activated, players will need to navigate to a gameâs store page where a check box will allow us to give the game as a gift. After checkout, you’ll select the player within your “family” to give the game, select from eight colors of digital wrapping paper, and the game will appear on their system.

There are notable limitations to this new feature. Supervised accounts within a family group are unable to gift games, and the number of games able to be purchased and gifted within an unspecified period is limited. Some games may also be unavailable for gifting, and any games gifted are immediately sent to the receiving player (so donât go buying those Christmas gifts early!). The feature, however, will be available to players both on the original Nintendo Switch as well as the Switch 2.

Itâs a welcome addition, though the inability to freely gift a game to anyone on your friends list (as seen on Steam) makes the feature bizarrely limited. Still, the fact its available at all is welcome for parents of gaming kids, or the found family of friends sharing a Nintendo Switch Online Family Plan.
