Exclusive: See Camila Morrone in Netflix’s Sexually Charged ‘The Age of Innocence’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

When Edith Wharton was in her mid-40s, she had a sexual awakening. For decades, the celebrated novelist had been stuck in a dull, loveless marriage to Edward Robbins Wharton, a fellow patrician 12 years older than her. Then she met debonair journalist William Morton Fullerton and fell—hard.

“I have drunk of the wine of life at last,” she wrote on May 21, 1908, in the document Wharton scholars call her “love diary.” “I have known the thing best worth knowing, I have been warmed through & through, never to grow quite cold again til the end.” Years later, she’d finally divorce Teddy and move abroad, where she’d channel these transformative experiences into her most celebrated novel: The Age of Innocence, published in 1920.

“I was at a place where I said I want to do anything except period, because I’ve done so much period,” says showrunner Emma Frost, whose résumé includes a trio of lavish historical series: The White Queen, The White Princess, and The Spanish Princess. But when Netflix asked if she might be interested in adapting The Age of Innocence, she reconsidered. Frost had read the novel and seen Martin Scorsese’s lush 1993 adaptation, starring Daniel Day-Lewis, Winona Ryder, and Michelle Pfeiffer. “I remembered it was the very first love story I ever engaged with that felt truly adult,” she says.

Then she learned that Wharton had actually written two alternate endings to her book—“and both the alternative endings have explicit sexual content.” It was a surprising development considering the published version of the novel, which is full of tortured yearning—there’s an achingly erotic moment concentrated on a character silently lifting a velvet ribbon to his lips—but contains no actual sex.

Her discovery led Frost to fall down a Whartonian rabbit hole. She learned everything about Wharton’s life-changing affair, devouring the 300 love letters the author had sent to Fullerton. “Once you actually delve into the Wharton archive, there’s all sorts of unpublished works that are explicitly about this experience of being a very sexual woman,” Frost says.

“The yearning, and the tension, and the longing, and the looks, and all of that delicious stuff is so important. I’ve been really obsessive in trying to capture that, and be very faithful to it.” But the sexy stuff in Wharton’s archive is what inspired Frost to make an Age of Innocence that’s not so innocent after all.

Ben Radcliffe as Newland Archer, Kristine Froseth as May Welland, and Camila Morrone as Ellen Olenska

As in the novel, her series—premiering on Netflix February 18—follows Gilded Age aristocrat Newland Archer, a gentleman lawyer preparing for a staid but satisfying life with his betrothed, well-bred ingenue May Welland. But his carefully planned future is thrown into disarray when May’s cousin, the independent-minded Countess Ellen Olenska, arrives in New York under a cloud of scandal, and with a highly unusual desire for this era: She wants to divorce her horrible husband.

Unlike the novel, Frost’s version also has nudity and graphic content: masturbation, voyeurism, a narratively important threesome. It’s not your grandmother’s Edith Wharton. But Frost would argue it’s very true to the spirit of its author.

And there’s more to her Age of Innocence than sex, of course. Wharton’s novel is told from Newland’s limited perspective; we only know what he knows, and see what he sees. Frost, though, wanted to make her version a true three-hander. “I think when you stay just with the male point of view, May becomes the antagonist, and Ellen becomes the thing to be won or saved or rescued. And that wasn’t something I wanted to do,” she says. “I wanted to have these two women really existing as flesh-and-blood women who we can care about.”

To accomplish this, she’d need a dynamic cast featuring The Witcher alum Ben Radcliffe as Newland, Kristine Froseth—who also stars in the Wharton-based Apple series The Buccaneers—as May, and Emmy nominee Camila Morrone as Ellen. Newland, says Frost, “doesn’t fit in with this society. He’s straining at the conventions. Ellen represents that other world, greater freedom”—the very freedom Wharton herself found when she ditched both New York City and her husband.

Radcliffe seemed to understand his character on a profound level. “There was a stillness that nobody else brought, and you could just see everything happening behind his eyes,” Frost says. Morrone, best known for playing a May Welland-esque character on Prime Video’s Daisy Jones and the Six, was similarly well-suited for hers: “She’s the most fearless actor I have ever met.”

Kristine Froseth as May Welland and Camila Morrone as Ellen Olenska

And then there’s May, who might be the trickiest member of the trio for modern audiences to understand. Unlike her fiancé and her cousin, she likes occupying a prescribed role in buttoned-up 1870s society—and she’ll do what she must to protect her perch, without malice or cruelty. Froseth found a way to convey the character’s depth and dimensionality.

“I always felt quite indignant on May’s behalf. All she ever did was fall in love with a man who courts her, say yes to a man who proposes to her, do everything right—and then along comes her cousin, and suddenly her choices are be shamed, be dumped, or fight for him,” says Frost. “Kristine just brings this light into the room. You love her, and you want her to be happy, and not to be disappointed. And that was the essential quality for me with May.”

There’s no real antagonist in Frost’s Age of Innocence. “I think it felt complex and morally right to keep saying, ‘There’s three people here that have feelings. What is the right thing in this situation?’” she says.

Kristine Froseth as May Welland and Ben Radcliffe as Newland Archer

Frost also wanted to flesh out the world of Wharton’s novel by adding mentions and subplots about real-world events its characters may have been aware of—like the Comstock Act of 1873, an anti-obscenity law that has never been repealed—and by introducing new settings like a boxing gym, where Newland goes to work out his aggression. In the series, that’s also where he befriends journalist Ned Winsett, a character from Wharton’s book reimagined here as a Black New Yorker played by Kel Matsena. “For me, it felt like a very organic thing to have non-white characters” in the series, says Frost. “They are the new ideas. They are the exciting conversations. They are the music that’s coming in.” Casting Argentine-American Morrone as the Countess Olenska, “making Ellen part of that influx, [also] felt right.”

As in any good period piece, the show’s costumes—designed by Daniel Orlandi, whom Frost calls a “bona fide genius”—and production design, overseen by Jean-François Campeau, are vital storytelling engines as well. “We always talked about these ridiculous little Victorians, trying so hard to believe they could live from the neck upwards—somehow divorce themselves from their animal nature and their bodies. The clothes and the sets were all part of that,” says Frost. “Weather and animals are really big motifs in the show. It’s about contrasting these people with the real, natural world and the weather that kind of goes, ‘Ha, ha, ha, you think you can somehow exist away from nature?’”

Camila Morrone as Ellen Olenska and Ben Radcliffe as Newland Archer

Though Frost and her team paid careful attention to detail, she cautions that sticklers may find things to quibble about if they examine the costumes carefully: “Our bustles are slightly smaller than they should be in the period.” And that’s without even considering the sexual aspect of the series. “Oh, there will be some pearl clutching,” Frost laughs. “Totally, I’m sure.”

But an expert in period drama like Frost knows when embracing historical accuracy actually matters, and when to let it slide. Wharton did too. While writing The Age of Innocence, Frost says, the author discovered that famed Swedish soprano Christina Nilsson did not actually sing Faust in New York in the year she'd set her novel—even though a performance of Faust starring Nilsson was the catalyst for her book’s action. Wharton wrestled with whether she should include the scene anyway. “And then she wrote to her sister-in-law and said, ‘I don’t care. Atmosphere is more important than historical accuracy.’”

“So I’m like, ‘Okay, Edith. If it’s good enough for you, it is good enough for me.’”

Viking legend informs ring-like viewpoint on Icelandic mountain ridge

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ring of Bjólfur is a mountain viewpoint in Iceland, designed by local studios Arkibygg Architects and Esja Architecture to evoke a giant silver ring left on the alleged burial site of a legendary Viking.

Located in Seyðisfjörður, a fjord in eastern Iceland, the viewpoint improves access to a ridge on Mount Bjólfur and celebrates its varied views over the town, valley and surrounding peaks.

Arkibygg Architects and Esja Architecture‘s design draws on the legend of Bjólfur, an Icelandic Viking settler believed to have claimed this landscape and been laid to rest on the mountain.

Local lore says Bjólfur was buried on the mountain with his most precious belongings, though they have never been found. The viewpoint, therefore, evokes a ring that has been left for him.

“The medieval Book of Settlements, or Landnámabók, tells of Bjólfur, the settler who first claimed land in Seyðisfjörður and gave the mountain its name,” Arkibygg Architects told Dezeen.

“The book says little about him, but local tales tell that he was laid to rest in a burial mound high up on the mountain,” it continued.

“By ancient custom, chieftains were often buried with their treasures, silver and jewellery. Bjólfur’s grave goods have never been found, but we let our imagination run free and left him a great silver ring.”

Arkibygg Arkitektar and Esja Architecture won a competition to design Ring of Bjólfur in 2021. The project was shortlisted in the small projects category of this year’s Dezeen Awards and also won the public vote for the same category.

Ring of Bjólfur takes the form of a perfect circle, measuring 32 metres in diameter. While emulating a ring, this was also designed to “work with views in every direction”.

Positioned on a mountain ridge 650 metres above sea level, the structure was built from post-tensioned concrete with stainless steel railings, chosen for durability and for colours that emulate silver. It partially cantilevers on one side.

“The ring can be used in many ways, and the view changes depending on where you are in the circle – and whether you are standing or sitting,” said Arkibygg Arkitektar.

“The choice of materials grew from the idea of creating a silver ring, while also having to withstand wind and weather at 650 metres, with the structure being submerged in snow for parts of the year,” said the studio.

A major focus for the studios was ensuring accessibility, creating “an experience that everyone can enjoy, including wheelchair users”.

To achieve this, they designed accessible parking next to the ring as part of the project. Further general parking is located further downhill to encourage people who are physically able to walk to the platform.

“The project has been very well received, not only by visitors but just as much by local residents,” it continued.

“The ring is already strengthening tourism in the town, and it has also become part of residents’ daily lives, widely used for outdoor recreation. People walk, cycle or even ski up there to take in the view.”

The landscape surrounding the viewpoint was preserved as much as possible, with any boulders that needed to be temporarily removed lifted back into place upon completion.

“The immediate surroundings – the soil and the rocks – matter a great deal. The aim was to disturb the ground as little as possible and to repair what was disturbed.

“Other areas affected by the works were restored with native vegetation. Having one clear destination also keeps visitors from wandering across the fragile ridge.”

Other viewpoints recently featured on Dezeen include an arrow-shaped structure in Denmark by Arup and the Lech Mountain Mirror by Snøhetta in Austria.

Meta Pauses Its Updated Business And Ad Terms Due Nov. 20 via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Guide: AI Max Drove 68% Of Retail Invalid Clicks

Updated business and ad terms won't take effect as scheduled. The ad terms still in force let the company crawl advertisers' sites, including for training.

Meta paused new ad and business terms scheduled to go into effect on Nov. 20. That leaves advertisers and other businesses using Meta’s services operating under existing terms. The pre-existing terms permit Meta to crawl content on advertiser websites for use such as “research and training,” whenever the advertiser places an ad order.

The Meta Business Help Center notice refers to the Commercial Terms, Product Catalog Terms, Self-Serve Ad Terms and Ad Creative Generative AI Terms. According to the notice, the updates “are now paused and will not take effect on November 20, 2026.”

On Nov. 5, Nieman Lab reported that Meta had posted the notice regarding halting the updates, and according to a Meta spokesperson, “We’re pausing the changes as we continue to engage with stakeholders and take our time to get this right.”

Meta says the four existing documents stay in effect until further notice. Each one’s page now opens with a line saying the updates are on hold. When I checked on Nov. 10, the U.S. and UK English previews of the updated Commercial Terms and Self-Serve Ad Terms showed a “page isn’t available” message.

The Commercial Terms currently in force, dated March 6, 2024, don’t include a crawling paragraph. The paused version would have added one covering sites associated with a business’s products or services. The Commercial Terms apply to any business use, including managing a Facebook Page.

The Self-Serve Ad Terms in force, dated Aug. 22, 2025, already include a crawling grant. By placing an ad order, an advertiser gives Meta the right to “access, index, cache, analyze or crawl” the content on its domains. The listed uses include developing advertising and other business-related products, “research and training,” and maintaining the integrity of Meta’s products.

The Ad Creative Generative AI Terms, dated May 6, 2024, also let Meta crawl URLs that advertisers provide, for use with their ads.

Adweek reported in October that the suspended Commercial Terms included a new section called AI Features. In this section, advertisers would be “solely responsible” for all of the results produced by Meta’s AI features as well as all changes those features make (including features that Meta enables automatically).

In its Ad Creative Generative AI Terms currently in effect, Meta has tied that responsibility to the advertiser’s own actions. The terms say that when an advertiser makes use of or posts AI-generated content, they are “solely responsible” for determining if the Output is correct, and if it is suitable for public display.

According to a Meta spokesperson who spoke to Adweek, advertisers can opt out of individual Advantage+ features at any time from within their Ads Manager account.

Businesses using Meta will not be required to consider any new business or ad terms prior to Nov. 20. For advertisers, the crawling allowance under the current ad terms will continue to apply when an advertiser places a new order; likewise, the terms granting this crawling allowance do not mention robots.txt.

The announcement from Meta says the company has made no changes to its crawling methods. Furthermore, the same options are available to content owners to express their crawl preferences via robots.txt.

Meta’s crawler documentation identifies Meta-ExternalAds for ads and various other business products. Additionally, the documentation identifies Meta-ExternalAgent for uses such as training foundation AI models. The documentation specifically includes robots.txt rules naming both of these crawlers, so review how you currently handle rules for both within your robots.txt file. The same page says two of Meta’s other crawlers might skip robots.txt in certain situations.

Meta says it’s still talking to the parties involved and that it’ll give an update once it has more to share. As of Nov. 10, the announcement hadn’t set a timeline for updating terms, nor had it specified whether or how much of the revised terms would be changed.

Nieman Lab reports that courts in Brazil and Norway have entered preliminary injunctions against these terms based on complaints made by press organizations. According to the UK’s Competition and Markets Authority (CMA), its investigation is still open. It is investigating both the current terms of business for Meta and proposed changes to those terms. The timelines listed are subject to revision; currently the first phase of fact-gathering is scheduled to run through January 2027.

Nintendo's Analysis Indicates You Might Want To Watch The Zelda Movie With Others

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda movie is fast approaching, scheduled to release on 30th April 2027 (we could really do with a trailer soon, Nintendo).

A such, we felt it only right to relay a new quote from Nintendo's latest financial Q&A session. While it may seem obvious to some (and perplexing to others), it seems that the best way to experience Nintendo's movies is to watch them with friends and/or family. In other words, if you want to have more fun, maybe don't go on your own.

This comes specifically from Yusuke Beppu, corporate director and member of the board at Nintendo. He explains that the company surveyed the satisfaction levels of viewers who watched The Super Mario Galaxy Movie and found that the levels "were higher when people watched in groups of two instead of alone, and higher still when watched in groups of four instead of groups of two".

"I want to mention that when we surveyed the satisfaction levels of people who watched The Super Mario Galaxy Movie, we found that these levels were higher when people watched in groups of two instead of alone, and higher still when watched in groups of four instead of groups of two. As illustrated by the four controller ports on Nintendo 64 in the past, Nintendo has long placed value on the idea of everyone playing together.”

Of course, despite Nintendo's findings, we want to stress that you're free to enjoy movies in whatever manner you deem suitable. Going to the cinema on your own can be a joyous experience, so if that's how you want to experience the new Zelda movie, then don't let Nintendo (or us!) convince you otherwise.

Nintendo also once again reiterated its desire to make more movies beyond Zelda. Beppu-san explained that movies "allow us to convey the appeal of Nintendo's uniquenessand characters in a concentrated 90- or 120-minute experience", while also providing an opportunity for its developers to learn new skills.

‘Definitely’: EFL expert has say on Birmingham City’s top two chances

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Birmingham City are sat top of the Championship table after their first 15 fixtures of the season. BCFC are above Middlesbrough and Stoke City on goal difference, with the likes of Wolves, West Brom and Swansea City also chasing them down.

The Blues are back in action after the international break with an away trip to the Bet365 Stadium to take on Stoke City as they aim to keep their momentum going. They beat Swansea City 2-1 at home last time out after a brace by Jay Stansfield.

Elek reckons the Midlands outfit can keep it up and has said on the Not The Top 20 Podcast on YouTube: “They can definitely stay there. There is no reason why they can’t. We’re not talking about a Championship minnow here. We’re talking about a club who have invested a lot of money in their playing squad. They were the best League One squad vs the level we’ve seen in a long time.”

He added: “The ascent Ipswich and Sunderland have shown, and to an extent Coventry although that took a few more seasons, the pathway is already there.”

Birmingham City came up from League One back in 2025 after they stormed to the title. BCFC then spent the last campaign adapting to life in the Championship and fell short of the play-offs.

The Blues stuck with head coach Chris Davies in the summer though and that decision has worked out well for them. They are rock solid at the back and they have the best defensive record in the division at the moment ahead of West Brom and Southampton.

Christoph Klarer and Phil Neumann have both been excellent at the heart of defence, with Alex Cochrane to the left of them. Dael Fry, who joined in the summer after the end of his contract at Middlesbrough, then provides useful back-up from the bench.

Further up the pitch, Jhon Solis, Paik Seung-ho and Tomoki Iwata inject quality into their midfield department. Stansfield, Liam Millar, Patrick Roberts, Demarai Gray and August Priske are all dangerous attackers and can cause serious issues to opposition defenders.

Birmingham City could do with another striker in the January transfer window. BCFC need some competition for Priske with Luis Vazquez struggling to get to grips with English football.

The Blues would also benefit from another attacking midfielder or winger ahead of the second-half of the season. Their pursuit of Joe Gelhardt in the summer was well documented and someone like him would be ideal, although he won’t leave Hull City.

You could also argue that Davies should bring in another central defender this winter too. If Klarer, Neumann or Fry were to get injured, they would be left pretty short of depth in that area.

Birmingham City are on a roll and will be full of confidence. Coventry City and Hull City showed last season that you don’t need parachute payments to go up.

The Blues need to stay grounded though and can’t get too carried away. Their away defeat at Cardiff City last month proved every single match is tough.

Ligue 2: ASSE, very bad news expected this Wednesday

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In Ligue 2, AS Saint-Étienne have won their six matches played at home. A series to which Saint-Etienne supporters are obviously no strangers. True to their reputation, the Magic Fans and the Green Angels increased the activities and songs to push the Greens to Geoffroy-Guichard.

On October 24, the Kop Nord notably marked the occasion on the occasion of its 35th anniversary. Imposing Tifos, smoke bombs and fireworks transformed the stands into a real spectacle. But these festivities were not to the taste of the Professional Football League. The disciplinary committee has decided to collectively sanction the Saint-Etienne supporters by pronouncing the closure of the Kop Nord for the next three home matches. A decision which deprives some 8,000 spectators of their usual stand.

The Kop Nord will therefore only reunite with its supporters on January 29, for the reception of Dunkirk. By then, the Greens will have played three matches in Geoffroy-Guichard: Laval, against which the sanction has already been served, then Nancy, on December 12, and Boulogne, on January 15. But the Kop Sud supporters also made a name for themselves during the reception of the Laval residents. Three tifos were deployed and smoke bombs were also lit in the stand.

Enough to attract, again, the attention of the disciplinary committee. Given the usual firmness of the LFP with regard to the use of pyrotechnic devices, the Green Angels in turn risk a sanction. The Kop Sud could thus be closed for at least two matches. If this hypothesis is confirmed, Geoffroy-Guichard’s two lungs would therefore remain silent until 2027, and the reception of Dunkirk. The verdict will fall this Wednesday, at the end of the traditional disciplinary committee.

Moonshot AI rented Nvidia’s top chips in China, Bloomberg reports

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Documents show a $65M deal for servers with B300 chips, which are barred from China.

Chinese AI start-up Moonshot AI signed a deal in August to rent servers fitted with Nvidia’s most powerful AI chips. The US does not allow these chips to be sold to China without a licence. The deal was revealed in financing documents seen by Bloomberg.

Moonshot, however, disputes that the documents are accurate, and the company said it has no business relationship with the company that owns the servers, and did not take part in or approve the financing.

According to the documents, the deal covers 32 Super Micro servers equipped with Nvidia B300 chips. They are kept in Qingyang, a city in western China that is one of the country’s national computing hubs.

The rental is worth 432m yuan ($64.8m), or about $2m per server. Bloomberg estimates that is roughly 350% more than the going rate in the US. B300 servers already sell at a premium in China.

The servers belong to Lingmeixin, a Beijing company whose website describes it as a distributor of DJI drones. To buy them, it took a 200m yuan ($30m) loan from Beijing Zhongguancun Bank. It used both the servers and its contract with Moonshot as security.

Lingmeixin bought the servers from a company that mainly sells alcoholic drinks, including the Chinese spirit Maotai, and has recently moved into AI. The documents do not show how it got hold of them.

After Bloomberg asked for comment, the records no longer mentioned Moonshot, and the media outlet also could not confirm whether anyone had obtained a US export licence.

Both US companies distanced themselves from the deal. Nvidia said the firms involved are not its customers and do not receive its support. Super Micro, for its part, said it does not do business with them and follows all US export rules.

Moonshot makes the Kimi AI models, and its Kimi K3 model is seen as one of China’s strongest. The company is planning to list in Hong Kong early next year.

The White House has already accused Moonshot of using banned Nvidia chips to train Kimi K3. Meanwhile, Washington has been mapping how China rents the chips it cannot buy, and trying to stop smuggling. Research firm SemiAnalysis estimates that hundreds of thousands of them have already got through.

The report adds to pressure on Nvidia this week. Its chief executive, Jensen Huang, has admitted an affair with a former Megaspeed executive whom US officials suspect of spying for China. Megaspeed is at the centre of a US chip-smuggling probe. Nvidia’s board has opened an independent investigation.

what does it take to enter design history? ADI answers with compasso d’oro

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

from digital feeds straight into permanent design history, adi opens submissions for the compasso d’oro 2027 at expo belgrade. the global call invites spatial, industrial, and interactive projects addressing human play to inhabit a world-stage pavilion.

mariana rodrigues November 10, 2026 3 min

What gives contemporary design international cultural weight? For Expo 2027 Belgrade, the Compasso d’Oro International Award suggests the answer lies in human play. Curated by ADI with the BIE under the theme ‘Design for Humanity at Play,’ the initiative calls for recently launched products, services, and interactive experiences. Moving from online submissions into tangible public space, shortlisted projects will inhabit a dedicated pavilion in Belgrade’s Best Practices district.

While the modern creative cycle often rewards ephemeral online impressions, true significance in architecture and industrial design is still measured by cultural permanence. The ADI Design Museum in Milan provides precisely that anchor, serving as an active custodian of the objects, systems, and spaces that define contemporary living. For participating creators and brands, selection secures a permanent place within the ADI Compasso d’Oro Historical Collection, establishing each project as an enduring reference point for future generations.

Operating within Expo 2027 Belgrade’s framework of ‘Play for Humanity – Sport and Music for All’, the competition repositions play from passive entertainment into a serious tool for human connection and collective wellbeing. The scope is broad, accepting realized work across nine diverse fields: from Lighting, Living, and Work to Mobility, Food, Individual Goods, Communication, Exhibition, and Social Systems. Whether scaling a zero-emission transit solution or reconfiguring communal furniture, submissions must demonstrate how intentional design turns the instinct of play into lasting social impact.

An international jury will select 20 Compasso d’Oro winners, alongside up to 50 Honourable Mentions and three International Career Awards. The results will be unveiled live at Expo 2027 Belgrade on May 24, 2027, kicking off an exhibition that runs through August 15. Across nearly three months, the pavilion will stage six thematic weeks of talks, workshops, and public events to keep the projects in active dialogue with international visitors.

CASSINA Maralunga sofaLAMBROgio by Makio Hasuike

Bournemouth star gets first England call-up after Ezri Konsa blow

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Konsa has been ruled out of Thursday clash with Croatia at Wembley, leading James Hill to be called up as a replacement

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Bournemouth defender James Hill has been called up to the England squad for the first time, with Ezri Konsa being ruled out of Thursday’s Nations League game against Croatia.

Arsenal star Konsa will not be available for the World Cup rematch with Croatia at Wembley in another hit to Thomas Tuchel’s defensive options, following the withdrawal of Marc Guehi, although it is hoped the 29-year-old will be back in contention to face Spain in Madrid on Sunday.

That has prompted Tuchel to give 24-year-old Hill, who has impressed for Bournemouth last season and has become a even more important member of a flying Cherries side this term, a maiden call-up.

A statement released by England said: “AFC Bournemouth defender James Hill has received his first call up to the England squad. The 24-year-old, who represented his nation at MU20 and MU21 level, has joined the Three Lions ahead of the forthcoming Uefa Nations League fixtures.

“Meanwhile, Ezri Konsa will not be in contention for Croatia at Wembley on Thursday as he continues to follow his own programme but will return to full training later this week before Sunday’s trip to Spain.”

As the statement says, Hill does have experience with England at youth level, making four appearances for the Under-20s between 2021 and 2022 after debuting when he played for Fleetwood, before winning a sole cap for the Under-21s away to Kosovo in June 2022.

He has played 15 times for the Cherries this season, being a staple of their team in both the Premier League and Europa League and helping them to eight straight wins in all competitions heading into the international break.

Meanwhile, Konsa has also featured 15 times for his club this season, admirably standing in for the injured William Saliba to form a fearsome partnership with Gabriel at the heart of Arsenal’s defence, following his £55m summer move from Aston Villa.

Last week, he picked up a knock to his knee during the 2-1 Champions League win over Slavia Prague and was subbed off for Cristhian Mosquera in the 61st minute, although Gunners boss Mikel Arteta insisted afterwards this was simply managing his minutes during a busy period.

In his press conference on Friday ahead of the weekend’s clash with Hull, Arteta brushed off any injury concerns, saying of Konsa: “He’s fine – he was feeling much better today. We took him out on Wednesday for a precaution, but yesterday and today he’s been feeling good.” Konsa would go on to play 90 minutes in the 1-0 victory over the Tigers.

England didn’t specify what his issue was that prompted the call-up of Hill but the Bournemouth man is the second late centre-back addition made by Tuchel after Guehi pulled out of the squad due to injury on Monday – having come off at half-time of Man City’s weekend defeat to Nottingham Forest – leading to the return of Newcastle defender Dan Burn.

It is the first time Burn has been in the Three Lions squad since the World Cup, after being left out for September and October’s games, and he provides another option at the heart of defence alongside Everton’s Jarrad Branthwaite, Bayer Leverkusen’s Jarell Quansah and Arsenal’s Ben White, although the latter has been used exclusively as a right-back by the Gunners this season.

Comment | People mock Renoir, but painting joy is no easy feat

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Purveyor of happiness: Pierre-Auguste Renoir’s Dance at the Moulin de la Galette (1876) © RMN-Grand Palais/Mathieu Rabeau

“These young people are enjoying life, the beautiful weather, the morning sun shining through the foliage; what do they care about the rest of humanity?” So wrote Georges Rivière, writer and champion of Pierre-Auguste Renoir, of the French painter’s Dance at the Moulin de la Galette (1876), one of the highlights of Renoir and Love at London’s National Gallery (until 31 January 2027). “They are happy,” Rivière added, “and these are the words that come to mind of all who see this charming painting. None has given me more pleasure than this one.”

“Beautiful”, “happy”, “pleasure”—these terms sit uncomfortably with what John Elderfield, former curator at New York’s Museum of Modern Art, described to the painter Howard Hodgkin as “the modern attraction to difficulty”. Paul Perrin, co-curator of the Renoir show, writes in its catalogue that the “blissful couples and air of happiness” in Renoir’s 1860s to 1880s paintings have made them “immensely popular” yet also prompted “contempt for their apparent lack of complexity and depth”. His assessment was newly confirmed by the critic Eddy Frankel, writing recently in The Guardian: “I’ve hated his work for decades.” For him, Renoir only appeals “when his nudes look like a super-psychedelic, ultra-horny version of Rubens”.

Renoir himself understood that milder emotions irked critics. “It’s hard to get people to admit that a painting can be great painting and still be happy,” he wrote. But love, as framed in this show, has multiple forms, among them empathy. Specialists suggest that Renoir was not ignorant of the “anguished or miserable” elements of life that Perrin describes as being essential to many conceptions of modernity. When Rivière wrote about young people dancing in ignorance of the rest of humanity, he didn’t need to add that happiness can be fleeting and is often most intensely felt at its briefest.

Renoir’s Dance in the Country (1883) © RMN-Grand Palais (Musée d’Orsay)/Hervé Lewandowski

The writings of Baudelaire, and the paintings of Renoir’s peers like Degas and Manet, tell us how visible hardship would have been at the time. And Renoir, unlike those two peers, grew up in working-class Paris, so had lived experience that gave him, as Rivière wrote, “a keen sense of pity” for the poverty of “Montmartre families”. But Renoir clearly felt that if he did want to create great, happy art, he had to avoid troubling subject matter: “There are enough annoying things in life for us not to make more of them,” he said.

Another factor in Renoir’s commitment to joy and play was his deep admiration for what he saw as the golden age of French painting: the Rococo. As Mary Morton writes in the Renoir and Love catalogue, the work of artists like Jean-Antoine Watteau and Jean-Honoré Fragonard “embodied graceful pleasure and a harmonious relationship between the human figure and nature”.

Renoir shares this infatuation with Rococo with a contemporary artist who I think has come closer than anyone else to creating a great art of happiness: Ragnar Kjartansson, whose exhibition Weekdays in Arcadia opens at the Goodwood Art Foundation in Chichester this month (26 November-12 April 2027). Kjartansson told me in 2017 that his fascination with the period lies in the “contrast of really intellectual minds and… silly-billy nothingness”. At Goodwood, he will show No Tomorrow (2022), a video installation that extends his 2017 dance collaboration with choreographer Margrét Bjarnadóttir and composer Bryce Dessner. When I saw the original ballet, in which a group of female performers dressed in jeans and white T-shirts dance with acoustic guitars for the duration of the piece, one moment made me instantly grin: arranged in a grid, every other dancer would take it in turns to leap into the air while the performer next to them stood still, prompting a joyous effect of corporeal effervescence. Kjartansson told me he and Bjarnadóttir felt No Tomorrow was “a Watteau painting in disguise”.

Kjartansson’s most famous piece, The Visitors (2012), a video installation in which he and his friends perform a song in different rooms of a half-derelict mansion in New York State, provokes a similarly ecstatic response from its viewers. But Kjartansson was conscious, perhaps with the reputation of artists like Renoir and of the Rococo period in mind, that he was “in danger of becoming the feel-good artist”. And indeed, part of what makes him such a compelling figure is that the light of his work always comes with copious amounts of shade. At Goodwood, he is interested in “the idea of encountering monuments to sorrow and shakiness in the woodlands”. And yet, I suspect he will find ways to make us smile amid the melancholy.

Achieving joy in art is difficult. Perhaps the most perceptive observer of this fact is one of Renoir’s artistic heirs, Henri Matisse, who spoke of the anguish behind the pleasure in his work: “this happiness, this light and this serene wisdom that [have] seemingly become mine are sometimes earned through the harshness of adversity”. Matisse and, I think, Renoir—like Kjartansson—knew that achieving happiness is a deadly serious business.

The show will begin at the Musée des impressionnismes Giverny before travelling to Guernsey's Candie Museum

An exhibition sheds refreshing new light on the artist’s development

A double bill at the Paris museum demonstrates how the artist went beyond “pretty little paintings”

'Renoir and Love', which opens this October in collaboration with the Musee d'Orsay and the Museum of Fine Arts, Boston, has already sold more than 22,000 tickets

HMRC chase the estate of late ex-England manager Sven-Goran Eriksson for £3.5MILLION in unpaid tax - because he was 'too busy with his ladies to save'

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By LUKE AUGUSTUS, ASSISTANT SPORTS EDITOR

Published: 06:19 EST, 10 November 2026 | Updated: 06:25 EST, 10 November 2026

Sven-Goran Eriksson's estate is being chased by HM Revenue & Customs (HMRC) for an outstanding £3.5million in tax owed.

It comes as a result of the estate's inheritance following Eriksson's death in August 2024 due to pancreatic cancer.

As reported by Daily Mail Sport last year, the former England manager reportedly had assets worth £4.8m but owed a staggering £7.25m to HMRC alone. He also reportedly had at least £1m outstanding in other debts.

In a bid to help reduce that outstanding debt, his family sold his old mansion called Bjorkefors last year - albeit at a substantial loss.

Eriksson is survived by his son Johan, 47, and daughter Lina, 39 - from his first marriage to Ann-Christine Pettersson - as well as partner Yaniseth Alcides. During his time as England manager he had a high-profile relationship with Nancy Dell'Olio.

Speaking to The Sun, Eriksson's lawyer Anders Runebjer has revealed that the estate has no money left to settle the debt - aside from £300,000 in a UK bank and £1m in a Cayman Islands tax haven. He also stated his focus is now to salvage any money for Eriksson's two children after the ex-Three Lions boss had spent his finances around football and women.

Ex-England manager Sven-Goran Eriksson's estate is being chased for £3.5million by HMRC

In August 2024, he passed away at the age of 76 following a battle with pancreatic cancer

The Swede is survived by his two children Johan, 47, and daughter Lina, 39

'Sven earned millions but was always too busy with his football and his ladies to manage money very well,' said Runebjer.

'I told the HMRC I'm not able to get the money in the Cayman Islands or his UK bank and said they're welcome to seize it.

'They replied that they couldn't do that so I told them there was nothing else left - so they won't get paid.

'Sven was regularly sent bills by the British taxman and paid promptly but they changed the rules and now say he still owes £3.5m plus interest after he passed away.

'They're welcome to take whatever they can get but I'm not giving them anything.

'My priority is trying to get whatever money I can that he left for his children - not the British taxman.'

As aforementioned, Eriksson's estate sold his seven-bed house in Torsby, in his native Sweden - located on the banks of Lake Fryken - but at a reduced price.

According to The Sun, the property was on the market for £2m but his family had to lower the price by £400,000 to attract any prospective buyers.

And they went on to add that an anonymous e-commerce businesswoman has now bought it for an undisclosed sum of at least £1.3m - meaning a potential £700,000 loss on market value alone. However, the loss is greater as Eriksson bought the place in 2002 for £4.5m - meaning the true value lost is around £3m.

Last year, Eriksson's former home in Sweden was sold as his family look to reduce debt

Eriksson lived in the property with his partner Yaniseth Alcides until his death

During his time as England boss, he was in a high-profile relationship with Nancy Dell'Olio

Selling the property was the latest thing Eriksson's family have done in a bid to ease the financial burden they have taken on since his death.

In March 2025, they were forced to auction off his memorabilia - such as a lunch invitation from the late Queen.

Also up for auction was his Armani suit from a 2006 World Cup match with his hand-written team notes still in the pocket.

The 349 items reported to have gone under the hammer for a combined total of £140,000 also include his BBC Coach of the Year trophy in 2001 and a Premier League Manager of the Month award from his time as Manchester City boss.

An Ivory Coast top signed with a special message by former forward Didier Drogba from Eriksson's time as national coach in 2010 was sold as well.

Eriksson was estimated to have received around £7m in pay-offs alone from England, Manchester City and Mexico during his managerial career.

The Swede, who managed England between 2001 and 2006, previously described himself as being 'stupid' with his money.

In one of his last ever interviews, he told The Guardian: 'I was never interested in money - I never took care of it and trusted people to do that for me. I was stupid not to control where the money went. I realised that when I didn't have any more cash!

'I still don't know what I have in the bank.'
